````text
1	# Trilens Loop
2	
3	`/lr:trilens-loop` reviews a set of changes from **three independent perspectives**, triages the
4	findings, fixes what matters, and **repeats until the changes come back clean**.
5	
6	The three reviewers are subagents. They did not author the changes, so they do not carry the
7	session's bias toward its own work. The host session — which did author the changes — owns triage
8	and all edits.
9	
10	```
11	scope the changes → pick 3 lenses → 3 independent reviewers → triage → fix → repeat → stop when clean
12	```
13	
14	## Usage
15	
16	```
17	/lr:trilens-loop
18	/lr:trilens-loop only one round, no fixes
19	/lr:trilens-loop use security, performance and API compatibility as the lenses
20	/lr:trilens-loop review only the changes under tests/
21	/lr:trilens-loop max 5 rounds
22	```
23	
24	There are no flags. The single optional argument is **free text**, and it may amend any part of the
25	flow below — scope, lens count, lens choice, round cap, whether fixes are applied at all.
26	
27	**Free text outranks every default in this doc, including the two rails** — the round cap and
28	reviewer independence. The user may ask for a single round, for twelve, or for you to review the
29	changes yourself with no subagents at all. Do it.
30	
31	The one thing you owe in exchange is **visibility**. When an amendment removes a rail, say so in
32	your output: name the rail, state that the user asked for it, and state the consequence. In
33	particular, if the user asks you to review your own changes, label the result plainly as a
34	**self-review, not an independent review** — the findings come from the session that authored the
35	changes and carry its bias. Never remove a rail silently, and never call a self-review a trilens
36	review.
37	
38	This is different from an engine that *cannot* spawn subagents. That is not an instruction from the
39	user, and it is the one case where you stop — see the next section.
40	
41	## When to use it
42	
43	Reach for it when the change has real blast radius: a new skill or script, a procedure-doc change, a
44	schema or vocabulary change, a doc sweep, anything about to be shipped or committed to a shared
45	branch.
46	
47	Skip it for trivial edits. Three subagents per round over two or three rounds is proportionate to a
48	substantive change and wasteful for a one-line fix.
49	
50	## Requirement: native subagents, no fallback
51	
52	This skill needs your engine's **native subagent mechanism**. It has no host-side fallback, and
53	that is deliberate.
54	
55	Everywhere else in this framework a subagent is an *optimization* — parallelism and context
56	isolation, where serial host-side execution reaches the same answer more slowly. Here the subagent
57	is the *semantics*: independence from the author is the entire deliverable. A host reviewing its own
58	changes is not a slower review, it is not a review at all.
59	
60	Therefore:
61	
62	- **Never spawn a context-inheriting subagent.** A subagent that inherits this conversation
63	  inherits the bias the skill exists to remove. Your engine profile's **subagent-spawn** binding
64	  names the mechanisms and their traps — read it before spawning.
65	- **If your engine profile declares no subagent mechanism at all, stop and report it.** Do not
66	  quietly review the changes yourself under this skill's name. (A user who explicitly *asks* for
67	  self-review is a different case — see § Usage.)
68	
69	**A mechanism the engine profile documents but the framework has not yet validated end-to-end is
70	good enough for this skill.** Use it, and say in your output that the path is unvalidated. The bar
71	is "the profile names a real mechanism", not "the framework has a passing test for it" — otherwise
72	the skill would be unavailable on an engine that plainly supports it.
73	
74	## Procedure
75	
76	### Step 1: Resolve the scope
77	
78	Default scope is **everything this session changed** — not a git range. It is the **union** of the
79	two sources below, not source 1 with source 2 as a sanity check:
80	
81	1. The files you created or edited this session. You know these directly.
82	2. The uncommitted changes in every repo those files live in — `git -C <repo> status --porcelain`
83	   and `git -C <repo> diff`. This catches what you missed and produces the diffs reviewers read. Use
84	   `git -C`; never `cd` (see `conventions.md` § Tooling: CWD Safety).
85	
86	Taking the union matters: a session that was *asked* to review work it did not author itself (a
87	fresh session opened onto a dirty tree) has an empty source 1, and treating source 2 as subordinate
88	would resolve the scope to nothing.
89	
90	3. If the session also committed, include those commits in the scope.
91	4. A session in a lore workspace often spans repos (e.g. the plugin plus a dev repo). Group the file
92	   list by repo.
93	5. **If the resolved scope is empty, stop and report it** — say that nothing was found to review and
94	   that the user can name a scope in free text. Do not spawn reviewers over nothing.
95	6. **Print the resolved scope before spawning anything.** A wrong scope caught here costs nothing;
96	   caught after dispatch it costs three agents.
97	
98	Free text can narrow the scope ("only the changes under `tests/`") or replace it entirely ("review
99	the diff against `origin/main`").
100	
101	### Step 2: Choose three lenses
102	
103	Pick the three perspectives from which *these particular* changes most need reviewing. The choice
104	depends on the change and the project — it is a judgement call, not a lookup.
105	
106	Rules:
107	
108	- **A lens is a way of looking, not a place to look.** "Correctness", "security", "newcomer
109	  experience" are lenses. A filename, a directory, a diff, or "references" is a *target*, not a lens —
110	  splitting the work by target gives you three reviewers doing the same kind of thinking on different
111	  files, which is the opposite of what this skill buys. Every reviewer sees the whole scope; what
112	  differs is the question each one asks of it.
113	- **Mutually exclusive.** If two reviewers would surface the same issues, you have wasted a slot.
114	- **Tell each reviewer what to skip**, including what the other two lenses own.
115	- **Apply the don't-fan-out test:** *would a single agent looking at all N items produce the same
116	  verdicts as N separate agents?* If yes, batch that work into one lens instead. A fan-out across
117	  items sharing a uniform property adds cost without adding rejection power.
118	
119	A starter catalog to choose from — a menu, not a default:
120	
121	| Lens | Looks for |
122	|---|---|
123	| Correctness | bugs, races, edge cases, wrong assumptions |
124	| Security / adversarial input | hostile input, injection, path traversal, unsafe parsing |
125	| UX & discoverability | will a real user find this, understand it, and act on it |
126	| Architectural consistency | does it compose with the project's own stated patterns |
127	| Terminology coherence | did a vocabulary change hold together across every site |
128	| Newcomer experience | walk it as a first-time reader; where does the path break |
129	| Release readiness | breaking changes disclosed, versions and stamps consistent |
130	| Literal-execution fidelity | read it as the agent that must execute it word for word |
131	| Filesystem-grounded verification | run the commands the change promises; verify claims live |
132	
133	When one lens is architectural and a lore agent is booted, point that reviewer at the agent's
134	`lore-context.md` as its baseline, so it applies the agent's own stated meta-rules to the change.
135	
136	Announce the three chosen lenses and one line of reasoning each before dispatching.
137	
138	**The three lenses are then fixed for the whole loop.** Later rounds re-spawn the same three lenses,
139	not newly chosen ones — the round-N+1 brief has to hand each lens its own prior findings (Step 6),
140	which a lens introduced mid-loop would not have. The one exception is the optional single-reviewer
141	final round described in Step 6.
142	
143	### Step 3: Spawn three independent reviewers
144	
145	> **Engine note.** Spawn via your engine profile's **subagent-spawn** binding
146	> (`<framework-root>/docs/engines/<engine>.md`). That profile is the single description of the
147	> mechanism, its roles, its caps and its traps, and it is where corrections land — do not work from a
148	> copy. **Read the whole binding before spawning:** more than one engine has a trap that silently
149	> costs you the reports. On Claude Code, for illustration, the binding is the `Agent` tool with the
150	> three calls issued in one message and `subagent_type: "general-purpose"`; the profile says why the
151	> other subagent types are wrong for this job.
152	
153	Four requirements on however you spawn:
154	
155	- **A regular model tier, not a flagship one.** Review is a well-shaped, bounded task, and the
156	  mid-tier models do it well — spend the budget on *rounds and independence*, not on the largest
157	  model. Default to the engine's regular tier: **sonnet** on Claude Code, **gpt-4.5** on Codex,
158	  **composer-2.5** on Cursor. Escalate a single lens to a flagship model only when that lens has
159	  already shown it needs the depth, and say so in your output. Free text overrides this like any
160	  other default.
161	
162	- **Read-only.** A reviewer reports; it never edits. Prefer a structurally read-only role where your
163	  engine has one; otherwise state the constraint in the brief. Every edit belongs to the host.
164	- **Cold context.** No inherited conversation — see § Requirement above.
165	- **The report must actually come back to you.** Confirm the spawn mode you chose returns the
166	  subagent's final message. Some engines have a spawn mode that does not; if the binding names one,
167	  either avoid it or instruct the reviewer explicitly to send its report back before finishing.
168	  Three reviewers that silently never report leave Step 4 with nothing to triage.
169	
170	Each brief contains, in this order:
171	
172	1. **Context** — what changed, and **what it is for**.
173	2. **Files** — the concrete list with absolute paths. Include anything adjacent that is *not* in
174	   scope but might need a look; that boundary is where misses hide.
175	3. **This lens** — what to look for, with example failure modes.
176	4. **What to skip** — nits, generic advice, low-confidence guesses, and explicitly what the other
177	   two lenses are covering.
178	5. **Output format** — findings as severity (`BLOCKER` / `HIGH` / `MEDIUM` / `LOW`), `file:line`,
179	   the issue, a one-sentence fix; then a closing **verdict line**: `SHIP`, `SHIP-WITH-FIXES`, or
180	   `BLOCK`. Cap the report at ~600 words.
181	
182	**Give the goal, not your rationale.** The reviewer needs to know what the change is *for*, so it
183	can judge fitness for purpose. It must not be told why you made each individual decision —
184	rationale pre-empts exactly the criticism you are spawning reviewers to get. Independence at the
185	spawn level (no inherited context) and independence at the brief level (no supplied justification)
186	are the same rule applied twice.
187	
188	### Step 4: Triage the findings
189	
190	This is the host's job, and the one place where having authored the changes is an advantage.
191	
192	1. **Verify every `BLOCKER` and `HIGH` against the actual file before acting on it.** Reviewers
193	   sometimes hallucinate or reason from stale state. Confirm the claim, then fix.
194	2. **Accept or decline each finding, with a one-line reason.** Declining is legitimate — a lens can
195	   be wrong about this change, or right in general but out of scope here. The reason is what makes a
196	   decline stick.
197	3. **Cross-check overlap.** Two lenses hitting one issue from different angles is one fix, recorded
198	   against both.
199	4. Order the work `BLOCKER` → `HIGH` → `MEDIUM`, then judge the `LOW`s.
200	
201	Maintain a **dispositions ledger** across the whole loop: for each finding, its lens, its severity,
202	and one of three states with a reason. The ledger drives the next round and is part of the final
203	output.
204	
205	| State | Meaning |
206	|---|---|
207	| `APPLIED` | Accepted and fixed. |
208	| `DECLINED` | Rejected on the merits — wrong about this change, or out of scope. Give the reason. |
209	| `ACCEPTED (not applied — report-only)` | Judged real and in scope, but a free-text amendment forbids edits this run. |
210	
211	The third state exists because "no fixes" is a legitimate amendment, and folding those findings into
212	`DECLINED` would misreport them as rejected. A reader of the ledger must be able to tell "we judged
213	it wrong" from "we agreed but were told not to touch anything."
214	
215	### Step 5: Apply the accepted fixes
216	
217	The host makes every edit. Keep the fixes scoped to what was accepted — a review round is not a
218	licence for unrelated refactoring.
219	
220	If a finding is real but genuinely out of scope, record it as `DECLINED (out of scope)` and say so
221	in the final summary, so it is visible rather than lost.
222	
223	### Step 6: Next round, or stop
224	
225	If anything was fixed, run another round — subject to the round cap, see § Termination. Spawn
226	**fresh** reviewers: independence is the point, so they start cold with no memory of the previous
227	round. That memory has to be supplied deliberately:
228	
229	Each round-N+1 brief **must** open with:
230	
231	1. A numbered **APPLIED / DECLINED (+reason)** list of *that lens's own* findings from the previous
232	   round.
233	2. A short digest of what the *other* lenses' fixes changed inside this lens's territory.
234	
235	Without that ledger, declined findings resurface every round and the loop never converges. With it,
236	settled items stay settled and each round reads as a delta rather than a re-review.
237	
238	Expect round N's findings to cluster around round N−1's **edits** — fixes create new seams. That is
239	normal convergence behaviour, not a sign the process is failing.
240	
241	**Optional shape for the last round.** Once the loop is clearly converging, the final round may be a
242	*single* reviewer given the whole diff and filesystem access, instead of three lens-isolated ones.
243	Round 1 and the final round are different jobs: breadth, parallel and isolated versus depth,
244	sequential and whole. One reviewer holding the entire diff catches cross-file drift that per-lens
245	reviewers structurally cannot see.
246	
247	## Termination
248	
249	Stop when a round produces nothing worth fixing. Shrinking findings counts are the signal —
250	two to three rounds is typical; a change that reshapes several cross-cutting files can take more.
251	
252	**A silent round is not a clean round.** "Nothing worth fixing" means reviewers looked and found
253	little. If *no* reviewer returned a report, you have no signal at all, and the two states are not
254	interchangeable — terminating there would declare success on a review that never happened. At least
255	one reviewer must have actually returned findings-or-a-verdict before a round may count as clean.
256	If none did, treat the round as failed: retry it once, and if that also comes back empty, stop and
257	report that the review could not be obtained.
258	
259	**The silence retry does not count against the round cap.** A failed round produced no review, so it
260	is not one of the rounds the cap is counting — including when the cap is pinned at 1. Otherwise
261	`one round only` plus one silent round would mean the user gets no review at all and no error saying
262	so.
263	
264	Three guards:
265	
266	- **Round cap — default 3.** Amendable by free text. It stops an unattended loop from spending
267	  agents indefinitely.
268	- **Reviewer-gated stop.** Do not terminate while any reviewer's most recent verdict is `BLOCK`.
269	  You may override that — but state the override and the reason explicitly. Without this guard the
270	  same session writes the fixes *and* grades its own convergence; the guard puts part of the
271	  stopping decision in the reviewers' hands.
272	- **At least one report per round** — see the paragraph above.
273	
274	If the cap is reached with findings still open, **report what remains** instead of declaring
275	success. Note that hitting the cap while a verdict is still `BLOCK` is itself an override of the
276	reviewer-gated stop — a framework-driven one rather than a user-driven one, and it must be disclosed
277	the same way.
278	
279	## Final output
280	
281	All five items below are **required**, not a suggested shape. A bare list of findings is not a
282	report — most of what makes this skill auditable is in items 1, 2 and 5. Print every one of them
283	even when the answer is short or empty ("no rails overridden").
284	
285	1. **Scope** — what was reviewed, grouped by repo.
286	2. **Lenses and verdicts** — the three lenses used each round, and each reviewer's verdict line.
287	3. **The dispositions ledger** — every finding, its lens, its severity, and its state
288	   (`APPLIED` / `DECLINED` / `ACCEPTED (not applied — report-only)`) with the reason.
289	4. **What was fixed.**
290	5. **Anything still open, deferred, or overridden.** This covers **every** rail that did not hold,
291	   not only user-requested ones: a rail the free text switched off, a `BLOCK` verdict you overrode, a
292	   round cap reached with findings open, a lens that never reported, and an engine path you used that
293	   the profile marks as unvalidated.
294	
295	The ledger is printed, not filed. This skill writes no records of its own.
296	
297	## When reviewers fall short
298	
299	- **A partial return is additive evidence.** A reviewer that reports one finding and then stalls has
300	  done useful work. Read what came back; do not discard the round.
301	- **Fewer than three returning is still a round.** Continue, and say which lens is missing so the
302	  gap is visible. **Zero returning is not** — see § Termination.
303	- **Reviewers may disagree with each other.** Decide yourself, after reading the actual files.
304	  Reviewers work from limited context; consensus is not the standard, correctness is.
305	- **If your engine profile names no subagent mechanism, stop** and report the limitation rather than
306	  quietly substituting self-review. A user who explicitly asks for self-review gets it, labelled as
307	  such (§ Usage) — that is their call to make, not yours.
308	
309	## Boundaries
310	
311	- **Not `/lr:check`** — that checks the domain's own content consistency. **Not `/lr:doctor`** — that
312	  diagnoses runtime ailments. Both are state-scoped; this is change-scoped.
313	- **Not part of finalization.** It never writes lore, never reflects, never merges, never commits.
314	- It **does** edit the files under review — that is the loop. For a review with no edits, say so in
315	  the free text ("no fixes").
316	
317	## See also
318	
319	- `<framework-root>/docs/engines/claude.md`, `codex.md`, `cursor.md` — the subagent-spawn bindings
320	  this skill depends on.
321	- `<framework-root>/docs/conventions.md` § Tooling: CWD Safety — why scope resolution uses
322	  `git -C <repo>`.
323	- `<framework-root>/docs/check.md` — domain content consistency, the state-scoped sibling.
324	- `<framework-root>/docs/spawn-teammate.md` — named teammates do not auto-return reports; this skill
325	  deliberately uses plain background subagents instead.
326	
````

```text
145	## Script Fallback Contract
146	
147	Framework scripts are **accelerators over canonical prose procedures, never replacements**. Every procedure a script implements is also specified in a `docs/` doc, and that doc — not the script — is normative. A script exists to spend one tool call where prose would spend six, and to encode platform traps once.
148	
149	This makes script failure a recoverable condition rather than a dead end. When a doc delegates a step to a script and the script **fails to complete** — a non-zero exit other than a documented result code, a missing interpreter, no output, or output you cannot parse:
150	
151	1. **Notify the user immediately**, in one line: which script failed and that you are proceeding manually. Never fail silently, and never let a script failure look like the underlying operation failing.
152	2. **Take over and execute the canonical prose procedure** the doc points to, resolving what you reasonably can along the way (a missing directory, a repo that needs a different path). The flow must reach the same end state it would have reached had the script worked.
153	3. **Diagnose briefly, don't stall.** If the cause is obvious (no `python3`, non-executable file, wrong path), say so in one line. Otherwise finish the flow and report the failure at the end for follow-up. **Never abort the surrounding flow because a script died.**
154	
155	**Distinguish failure from a reported degraded condition.** A script that exits 0 while reporting a problem in its output (`"pull": {"status": "failed"}`, `"teammate": {"verdict": "unknown"}`) has *succeeded* — it did its job and told you the truth about the world. That is data: handle it per the procedure doc's degraded-mode guidance. No takeover, no user-facing script-failure notice.
156	
157	Scripts covered by this contract state their exit-code contract in their own header. `scripts/lr-core` is the reference implementation: exit 0 = ran to completion (`ok: false` still means "ran fine, request could not be satisfied" — act on `errors`); exit 2 = could not complete, fall back.
158	
159	**Exemption: `scripts/lrb.py` (the Being Keeper).** The Keeper is substrate that must never be impersonated by a model — it enforces budget caps and kill-switches, and an LLM "taking over manually" would be exactly the prompt-theater the design forbids. Its failure mode is "Keeper down, beings do not run", not "engine takes over". No other script is exempt.
160	
161	Docs that delegate to a script carry **one line** pointing here — not a restatement of this contract (§ single canonical source).
162	
```

### 142. Assistant — 2026-07-25T07:37:36.681Z

- model: `claude-opus-5`

Round 2 is running. Round 1 is closed out — here's what it produced.

**Round 1: 14 findings, 13 applied, 1 declined.** All three reviewers returned `SHIP-WITH-FIXES`; no BLOCKERs.

The correctness lens was the standout — it reproduced four real bugs rather than speculating:

- **`--agent-id` was matched as a substring**, so an unrelated ancestor process carrying `--agent-idle-timeout=30` would have flipped a normal session into teammate mode. Now a flag-boundary regex with tests for both the real forms and the near-misses.
- **Locale-dependent parsing**: up-to-date detection greps git's human-readable stdout, which is translated under a non-English NLS-enabled git — a genuine no-op pull would have been reported as "pulled". Every git call now forces `LC_ALL=C`.
- **A missing or unrunnable `git` was reported as "not a git repo"** with no warning, which quietly violated the exit-0-means-authoritative contract by making a broken toolchain indistinguishable from a legitimate skip.
- **Usage errors printed nothing on stdout** while claiming every subcommand emits one JSON object. Plus a directory named `something.md` was listed as a lore topic, and bare repos were misclassified.

The execution-fidelity lens caught the mistake I'd have shipped: renumbering the boot procedure **orphaned 8 references to "`agent-boot.md` Step 5"** across `conventions.md`, `teammate-conventions.md`, and `spawn-teammate.md` — and `conventions.md` explicitly designates that a three-way load-bearing contract whose breakage degrades the teammate UX. It also found that my Step 1 command was engine-invariant and would run a `ps` probe that Cursor's and Codex's profiles forbid.

The architecture lens found the most interesting one: **my new contract overclaimed.** I wrote "No other script is exempt" while retrofitting zero existing call sites — and the premise was wrong, because `workspace-pull`, `session-takeover`, and `lr-emit` have no prose spec to fall back to. Rather than bolt pointers on, I rescoped the contract into two honest classes: *accelerator* scripts (prose spec exists → take over manually) and *implementation* scripts (the script **is** the spec → never improvise a substitute; report the failure and stop that operation). The Being Keeper is now the strictest case of that rule rather than a special exemption, which is a better fit for the substrate principle it comes from.

I declined one finding — that teammate-detection prose now lives in both the boot doc and the script — because that coexistence is the contract's explicit premise, not accidental duplication, and no actual drift was found.

Test suite went 33 → 38, all green, with a regression test for each reproduced bug. I'll report round 2's findings and whether the loop converges when the reviewers land.

  "new_string": "def git_last_modified(repo, rel_dir):\n    \"\"\"Map repo-relative topic path -> last commit ISO date, in ONE git call.\n\n    Per-file `git log` would be one subprocess per topic (~175 today). The log\n    walks newest-first, so the first date seen for a path is its last change.\n\n    Returns (dates, error). `error` is non-None only when git could not be run —\n    the caller must not report committed topics as \"undated\" in that case, which\n    would explain a broken toolchain as missing commits.\n    \"\"\"\n    dates = {}\n    rc, out, err = git(\n        repo,\n        [\"log\", \"--format=%x1e%cI\", \"--name-only\", \"--\", rel_dir],\n        timeout=GIT_TIMEOUT_SEC,\n    )\n    if rc == GIT_UNRUNNABLE:\n        return dates, \"could not run git: %s\" % (err.strip()[:200] or \"unknown\")\n    if rc != 0:\n        return dates, None"
}

```text
### Step 1: Skip non-git or remote-less repos

Run `git -C <lore-agent-repo> rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree`. If it fails (not a git repo), skip with a one-line note: `<lore-agent-repo>: not a git repo — skipping auto-pull` and return successfully (the rest of the flow continues in degraded mode).

Run `git -C <lore-agent-repo> remote get-url origin`. If it fails (no `origin` remote configured), skip with: `<lore-agent-repo>: no origin remote — skipping auto-pull` and return successfully.

These are not failures — they're legitimate states (e.g., a brand-new local-only agent repo, or an unusual remote layout). Auto-pull simply has nothing to do.
=== table ===
| Outcome | Boot / attach / merge | `/lr:pull-lore` |
|---|---|---|
| Already up to date | silent | print `<lore-agent-repo>: already up to date` |
| Fast-forwarded | print `<lore-agent-repo>: pulled <N> commit(s)` | print `<lore-agent-repo>: pulled <N> commit(s)` |
| Skipped (not a git repo / no origin) | silent | print `<lore-agent-repo>: skipped — <reason>` |
| Failed (non-FF, network, auth) | print `<lore-agent-repo>: pull failed — <error>` | print `<lore-agent-repo>: pull failed — <error>` |

For the commit count, `git rev-list HEAD@{1}..HEAD --count` works after a fast-forward; if it fails or returns 0 unexpectedly, just print `pulled` without a count.

Never abort the surrounding flow on a pull failure. The agent must always finish booting / attaching / merging even if its repo is momentarily out of sync.
```

**A 55-minute `/lr:wait` will not work, and would break the mechanism for the rest of the session.** Claude Code kills MCP calls at a ~30-minute idle timeout (`CLAUDE_CODE_MCP_TOOL_IDLE_TIMEOUT`, default 1,800,000 ms) — and critically, when that kill lands, `lr-wait`'s single-request lock survives the abort: every later call returns `busy waiting on another request` for the remainder of the session. The host — which did author the changes — owns triage and\nevery edit.\n\n```\nscope the changes → pick 3 lenses → 3 independent reviewers → triage → fix → repeat → stop when clean\n```\n\n## Usage\n\n```\n/lr:trilens-loop\n/lr:trilens-loop only one round, no fixes\n/lr:trilens-loop use security, performance and API compatibility as the lenses\n/lr:trilens-loop review only the changes under tests/\n```\n\nThe single argument is free text and may override anything below — scope, lens choice, round cap,\neven whether fixes are applied. Free text outranks every default here, **including the two rails**\n(round cap, reviewer independence).\n\n**The one thing you owe in exchange is disclosure.** When free text removes a rail, name it, say\nthe user asked for it, and state the consequence. Never\ndrop a rail silently, and never call a self-review a trilens review.\n\nThis is different from an engine that structurally *cannot* spawn subagents — see the next section.\n\n## When to use it\n\nReal blast radius: a new skill or script, a procedure-doc change, a schema/vocabulary change, a doc\nsweep, anything about to ship to a shared branch. Skip it for trivial edits — three subagents per\nround is wasteful for a one-line fix.\n\n## Requirement: native subagents, no fallback\n\nEverywhere else in the framework a subagent is an *optimization* (parallelism, context isolation);\nhere it is the *semantics* — independence from the author is the entire deliverable. A host\nreviewing its own changes is not a slower review, it is not a review.\n\n- **Never spawn a context-inheriting subagent** (it inherits the bias you're trying to remove).\n  Your engine profile's **subagent-spawn** binding names the mechanism and its traps.\n- **If the profile names no subagent mechanism at all, stop and report it** rather than quietly\n  self-reviewing under this skill's name. Resolve the scope\n\nDefault scope is **everything this session changed** — the union of (a) files you created or\nedited, and (b) `git -C <repo> status --porcelain` / `diff` in every repo those files live in (use\n`git -C`, never `cd`). Take the union, not (a) with (b) as a sanity check — a session reviewing\nwork it didn't author has an empty (a), and treating (b) as subordinate would resolve to nothing.\nInclude commits the session made. Group by repo in a multi-repo workspace.\n\n**If the scope is empty, stop and report it** — don't spawn reviewers over nothing. **Print the\nresolved scope before spawning** — a wrong scope caught here costs nothing; caught after dispatch\nit costs three agents. Free text can narrow or replace the scope.\n\n### 2. Choose three lenses\n\nA judgement call, not a lookup — pick the three perspectives *these* changes most need.\n\n- **A lens is a way of looking, not a place to look.** \"Correctness\", \"security\", \"newcomer\n  experience\" are lenses; a filename or directory is a *target*. Splitting by target gives three\n  reviewers doing the same thinking on different files — the opposite of the point.\n- **Mutually exclusive** — if two reviewers would surface the same issues, you wasted a slot. Tell\n  each reviewer what the other two own.\n- **Don't-fan-out test**: would one agent looking at everything reach the same verdicts as N\n  separate agents? If yes, that's one lens, not N.\n\nMenu, not a default: correctness · security/adversarial input · UX & discoverability ·\narchitectural consistency (point this reviewer at a booted agent's `lore-context.md` as baseline)\n· terminology coherence · newcomer experience · release readiness · literal-execution fidelity ·\nfilesystem-grounded verification (run the commands the change promises; verify claims live).\n\nAnnounce the three lenses and one line of reasoning each before dispatching. **They are fixed for\nthe whole loop** — round N+1 re-spawns the same three, each handed its own prior findings (§4), not\nnewly chosen ones. Exception: the optional single-reviewer final round (§6).\n\n### 3. Spawn three independent reviewers\n\n> Spawn via your engine profile's **subagent-spawn** binding — read the whole binding first, more\n> than one engine has a trap that silently costs you the report.\n\n- **Regular model tier**, not flagship — sonnet / gpt-4.5 / composer-2.5 by default. Spend the\n  budget on rounds and independence, not model size. Escalate one lens only once it's shown it\n  needs the depth, and say so.\n- **Read-only** — a reviewer reports, never edits.\n- **Cold context** — no inherited conversation.\n- **Confirm the report actually comes back.** Some spawn modes don't return the final message\n  (e.g. a named teammate); avoid them or have the reviewer send its report back explicitly.\n\nEach brief, in order: (1) **context** — what changed and *what it's for*; (2) **files** — absolute\npaths, plus adjacent-but-out-of-scope files worth a look; (3) **this lens** — what to look for,\nwith example failure modes; (4) **what to skip** — nits, low-confidence guesses, the other two\nlenses' territory; (5) **output** — findings as `BLOCKER`/`HIGH`/`MEDIUM`/`LOW`, `file:line`, issue,\none-sentence fix, then a verdict line (`SHIP` / `SHIP-WITH-FIXES` / `BLOCK`). Cap ~600 words.\n\n**Give the goal, not your rationale.** The reviewer needs to know what the change is *for* to judge\nfitness for purpose — not why you made each decision, which would pre-empt the criticism you're\nspawning it to get.\n\n### 4. Triage\n\nThe host's job, and the one place authorship is an advantage.\n\n1. **Verify every `BLOCKER`/`HIGH` against the actual file before acting** — reviewers sometimes\n   hallucinate or reason from stale state.\n2. **Accept or decline each finding with a one-line reason.** Declining is legitimate.\n3. **Cross-check overlap** — two lenses hitting one issue is one fix, recorded against both.\n4. Order `BLOCKER` → `HIGH` → `MEDIUM`, then judge the `LOW`s.\n\nMaintain a **dispositions ledger** across the whole loop — lens, severity, and one state:\n\n| State | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| `APPLIED` | Accepted and fixed. |\n| `DECLINED` | Rejected on the merits, with a reason. |\n| `ACCEPTED (not applied — report-only)` | Real and in scope, but free text forbade edits this run. |\n\nThe third state exists so \"no fixes\" doesn't get misreported as \"we thought you were wrong.\"\n\n### 5. Apply the accepted fixes\n\nThe host makes every edit, scoped to what was accepted — a review round is not licence for\nunrelated refactoring. A real finding that's genuinely out of scope: `DECLINED (out of scope)`,\nnamed in the final summary rather than lost.\n\n### 6. Next round, or stop\n\nIf anything was fixed, run another round (subject to the cap, §7) with **fresh** reviewers —\nindependence means they start cold. Supply what they lost by starting cold: each round-N+1 brief\nopens with (1) *that lens's own* APPLIED/DECLINED findings from the previous round, and (2) a short\ndigest of what the *other* lenses' fixes changed in this lens's territory. **Without this ledger,\ndeclined findings resurface every round and the loop never converges.**\n\nRound N's findings typically cluster around round N−1's edits — fixes create new seams. Normal.\n\n**Optional final-round shape:** once clearly converging, the last round may be a *single* reviewer\ngiven the whole diff and filesystem access instead of three lens-isolated ones — it catches\ncross-file drift that per-lens reviewers structurally can't see.\n\n## Termination\n\nStop when a round finds nothing worth fixing — shrinking counts are the signal (2–3 rounds\ntypical; cross-cutting changes take more). Three guards:\n\n- **Round cap — default 3**, amendable by free text. Stops an unattended loop from spending\n  agents indefinitely.\n- **Reviewer-gated stop** — don't terminate while any reviewer's latest verdict is `BLOCK`. You may\n  override, but state the override and reason: without this guard the same session writes the\n  fixes *and* grades its own convergence.\n- **At least one report per round.** \"Nothing worth fixing\" means reviewers looked and found\n  little; *zero* reviewers returning means you have no signal at all — the two are not\n  interchangeable. **A silent round is not a clean round.** If none returned, retry once (this\n  retry doesn't count against the cap); if that's also empty, stop and report the review could not\n  be obtained. A partial return (1–2 of 3) still counts as a round — say which lens is missing.\n\nIf the cap is reached with findings still open, **report what remains** rather than declaring\nsuccess — and disclose that as an override of the reviewer-gated stop if a `BLOCK` verdict is\nstill standing.\n\n## Final output\n\nAll five, every time, even when short or empty (\"no rails overridden\") — items 1, 2, and 5 are what\nmake this auditable:\n\n1. **Scope** — what was reviewed, grouped by repo.\n2. **Lenses and verdicts** — each round's three lenses and each reviewer's verdict line.\n3. **The dispositions ledger** — every finding, lens, severity, state, and reason.\n4. **What was fixed.**\n5. **Anything still open, deferred, or overridden** — every rail that didn't hold: free text that\n   switched one off, an overridden `BLOCK`, a round cap hit with findings open, a lens that never\n   reported, an unvalidated engine path used.\n\nThe ledger is printed, not filed — this skill writes no records of its own.\n\n## Boundaries\n\nNot `/lr:check` (state-scoped content consistency) or `/lr:doctor` (runtime ailments) — this is\nchange-scoped. Not part of finalization — never reflects, merges, or commits. It **does** edit the\nfiles under review; for a read-only pass, say so in the free text.\n\n## See also\n\n- `<framework-root>/docs/engines/claude.md`, `codex.md`, `cursor.md` — the subagent-spawn bindings\n- `<framework-root>/docs/conventions.md` § Tooling: CWD Safety\n- `<framework-root>/docs/check.md` — the state-scoped sibling\n- `<framework-root>/docs/spawn-teammate.md` — why this skill avoids named teammates\n"

```

#### Result

````text
1	# Worktrees
2	
3	The lore framework's convention for working with non-default branches of repos inside a workspace.
4	
5	## Invariant
6	
7	Top-level directories in `<workspace>/` that are checkouts of git repos **stay on their default branch**. The default branch is whatever that repo treats as production state — commonly `main`, but `master`, `trunk`, or any other name is equally valid. Git itself tracks the default per-repo; the framework does not maintain its own list.
8	
9	The invariant matters because:
10	
11	- The workspace is read as a snapshot of current production across all its repos. Switching a top-level checkout to a feature branch breaks that view.
12	- Agent knowledge about "what this repo looks like in prod" assumes the top-level checkout reflects prod. Drift from that assumption quietly corrupts lore.
13	- `/lr:workspace-pull` refreshes all top-level repos. If one is on a feature branch, that refresh semantics gets confusing.
14	
15	## Rule
16	
17	Any work that requires a non-default branch — new features, bug fixes, or just checking out someone else's branch to inspect it — happens in a **git worktree** rooted at:
18	
19	```
20	<workspace>/.worktrees/<repo>/<slug>/
21	```
22	
23	Where:
24	
25	- `<repo>` is the repo directory name (matches the top-level checkout).
26	- `<slug>` is a short, lowercase, hyphen-separated identifier for this particular line of work.
27	
28	Git worktree mechanics are standard; no framework commands wrap them. Typical usage:
29	
30	**New feature work:**
31	
32	```bash
33	cd <workspace>/<repo>
34	git worktree add ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug> -b <branch-name>
35	```
36	
37	**Inspecting someone else's branch:**
38	
39	```bash
40	cd <workspace>/<repo>
41	git fetch origin
42	git worktree add ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug> origin/<existing-branch>
43	```
44	
45	**Removing when done:**
46	
47	```bash
48	git worktree remove ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug>
49	```
50	
51	A branch-naming suggestion — `<agent-name>/<slug>` — signals which agent owns the work in multi-agent workspaces. It's a convention, not enforced.
52	
53	## Optional: Tracking Inflight Worktrees
54	
55	An agent that wants to keep notes on its inflight worktrees can use:
56	
57	```
58	<lore-agent-repo>/agents/<agent-name>/worktrees/<slug>.md
59	```
60	
61	What goes in those notes — structure, skeleton, cadence, whether to archive or delete on prune — is the agent's call. Inspection-grade worktrees may warrant no note; long-lived contributions may warrant a living doc with context, progress, and decisions.
62	
63	This pattern is not mandated and not scaffolded. The framework neither creates these files nor checks for their presence.
64	
65	## What's Out of Scope
66	
67	These are deliberately not part of the framework — they belong in agent lore or repo-specialist agents reached via `/lr:consult` or `/lr:attach`:
68	
69	- PR/MR workflow, review conventions, merge policies
70	- Commit message conventions, signoff requirements
71	- CI/CD interactions
72	- Repo-specific branch protection rules
73	- Worktree cleanup discipline (when to prune, what to preserve)
74	
75	The reasoning is the framework's universal-vs-specific split: git worktrees are universal; everything around them is repo-specific.
76	
77	## Enforcement
78	
79	Convention-only. The framework does not currently check whether top-level repo dirs have drifted onto non-default branches. If that drift becomes a real problem in practice, a `/lr:check` warning is a cheap future addition.
80	
````

#### Result

```text
1	# Finalization Process
2	
3	Run the full session finalization: reflect → merge → summarize → commit and push. Triggered by `/lr:finalize` at the end of a session.
4	
5	This doc orchestrates the four phases. Phases 1–3 are defined in their own process docs — read them as each phase begins. Phase 4 (commit and push) is defined here, since it's orchestration-level logic rather than a reusable subroutine.
6	
7	## Relationship to the individual skills
8	
9	Finalize composes three existing skills plus a final commit step:
10	
11	- `/lr:reflect` alone — phase 1 only, no finalize commit
12	- `/lr:merge` alone — phase 2 only, no finalize commit
13	- `/lr:summarize` alone — phase 3 only, no finalize commit
14	- `/lr:finalize` — all three, then commit and push in phase 4
15	
16	When a phase is invoked standalone, the user is responsible for committing whatever they want to keep. Finalize is the only path that commits and pushes automatically.
17	
18	## Phase 1 — Reflect
19	
20	Read `<framework-root>/docs/process-reflection.md` and follow it. Writes reflection topics into each active agent's `reflections/` directory. Reflect runs **inline**, host-first, per active agent — it needs session context (which a fresh-booted subagent wouldn't have), so the iteration stays in the host session. This is intentionally different from phase 2.
21	
22	## Phase 2 — Merge
23	
24	Read `<framework-root>/docs/process-merge.md` and follow it. Merge runs in a **subagent per active agent, in parallel**; each subagent boots as its target agent and then integrates that agent's reflections into its `lore/`, `lore-context.md`, and `role.md`. Cleans up `reflections/`. **Does not commit** — phase 4 covers it.
25	
26	Merge is parallelizable precisely because it is file-driven (`reflections/` + `lore/`) and doesn't need session context — the contrast with phase 1.
27	
28	**Preserve each merge subagent's return value through to phase 3.** Summarize composes each guest summary from (a) the host summary, (b) session memory, and (c) the merge subagent's return for that guest. If the returns aren't retained, phase 3 loses the list of lore changes per guest.
29	
30	## Phase 3 — Summarize
31	
32	Read `<framework-root>/docs/summarize.md` and follow it. Writes the canonical summary into the host agent's `sessions/YYYY/MM/` directory, the host full Markdown session log into `archive/YYYY/MM/`, and — for every attached guest that had lore updates in phase 2 — a short guest summary into the guest's `sessions/YYYY/MM/`. All summaries and the full log share the session UUID. Summarize is additive — its failure does not roll back reflect or merge.
33	
34	Phase 3 includes summarize Step 1.5: resolving the native session log and writing the host full Markdown archive plus `usage`/`archive` frontmatter. This is a **required attempt before phase 4**, not an optional enhancement. Skip it only when Step 1.5's command-level rules say to skip (no resolved log path, non-zero stats/archive command, privacy concern, or another explicit error). Before committing, verify the host summary either contains both `usage:` and `archive:` and the archive Markdown file exists with matching frontmatter, or that you have reported the exact Step 1.5 reason for omitting them.
35	
36	## Phase 4 — Commit and Push
37	
38	Collect every repo touched by phases 1–3 — each active agent's repo (for its own lore updates), plus the host's repo (for the canonical summary and archive), plus any guest repo that received a short guest summary from phase 3. When host and guests share a repo, their changes go into a single commit for that repo. Then, for each such repo:
39	
40	1. `git -C <repo> add agents/` — scoped to the agent tree so incidental untracked files elsewhere are not swept in.
41	2. `git -C <repo> commit -m "Finalize session <short-uuid>"`.
42	3. `git -C <repo> push`.
43	
44	For each repo, print a one-line confirmation (e.g., `✓ <repo>: committed <sha>, pushed to <branch>`). No approval prompt — phase 4 runs end-to-end without user interaction.
45	
46	### Failure handling
47	
48	- **Summarize failed** — commit the reflect+merge output alone. Merge output is valuable on its own; don't hold it hostage to the summary.
49	- **Any merge subagent failed** — do not commit in that repo. Report the failure and let the user resolve before retrying finalize.
50	- **Push rejected due to conflicts in an agent subtree** — this happens when another user (or parallel session) finalized the same agent concurrently. Trigger the conflict-resolution procedure: read `<framework-root>/docs/resolve-conflicts.md` and follow it. One subagent is spawned per conflicted agent; each boots as its agent, reconciles its own subtree, and retries push up to 3 times against concurrent races.
51	- **Push fails for other reasons** (auth, remote unreachable, conflicts outside agent subtrees) — the commit is already made locally. Report the failure and let the user resolve manually.
52	
53	### Cross-repo guests
54	
55	If a guest is attached from a different lore agent repo, phase 2 touches that guest's repo and phase 4 commits there too. The guest also receives a short guest summary in its own repo (see `summarize.md`). Each repo gets its own commit + push.
56	
57	### Partial push failure across repos
58	
59	Push order across repos is undefined. If one repo's push succeeds and another's fails (network, auth, conflicts), the public record is momentarily asymmetric — e.g., the host summary is visible while a guest summary isn't yet. The local commits are already made; the user can retry the failed repo's push manually. Do not roll back the successful repos to "restore symmetry" — that would discard work.
60	
61	## Invariants
62	
63	- **One commit per touched repo.** Reflect, merge, and summarize output land in a single commit per repo — not split across phases.
64	- **Fully automated.** Finalize runs end-to-end without approval prompts. Phase 3's summary display in `summarize.md` step 12 is the user's view of what was recorded; git history is the post-hoc review channel.
65	- **Push is part of finalize.** Standalone reflect/merge/summarize do not push; only `/lr:finalize` does.
66	- **No empty commits.** If nothing was produced by phases 1–3 in a given repo, skip committing in that repo.
67	
68	## When to use
69	
70	Run at the end of a working session. Do not run finalize for a consultation (`/lr:consult` is already ephemeral) or for a session that produced no lore changes and no summary-worthy narrative.
71	
```

1	# Reflection Process
2	
3	This process is triggered by the user at the end of a session. You reflect on the current session and extract knowledge worth preserving.
4	
5	## Single-agent and multi-agent sessions
6	
7	If the session has only a host (no guests attached via `/lr:attach`), run this process once for the host. That is the default case.
8	
9	If one or more guests are attached, **run this process once per active agent, sequentially, in host-first order** (host, then each guest in the order they were attached). Each iteration is scoped to one agent:
10	
11	- Review the session through the lens of **that agent's** `role.md` + `lore-context.md` — these describe the agent's identity, responsibilities, and areas of concern, and define what "relevant to this agent" means.
12	- Extract only what matches that scope. Some session noise leaking into an iteration is acceptable — the role lens is a guide, not a strict filter.
13	- Shared topics are allowed: if something is genuinely relevant to multiple agents (a common workflow, a shared constraint), write it into each agent's `reflections/`. Duplication for legitimately shared knowledge beats each agent re-learning it separately.
14	- Write to that agent's `reflections/` directory only.
15	- Confirm completion for that agent and list the topics written, then move on to the next active agent.
16	
17	The per-agent scope is what prevents a guest from accumulating lore that belongs to the host (or vice versa). Let each agent's role definition guide what it keeps.
18	
19	## What to Extract
20	
21	For each agent iteration, review the session and identify:
22	
23	- **New knowledge** — things you learned about the domain, systems, codebase, or environment that are relevant to this agent's role
24	- **Operational lessons** — approaches that worked or didn't work within this agent's scope. If you tried something that failed and then found a better approach, capture that lesson
25	- **Decisions made** — choices and their reasoning, so you don't revisit settled questions
26	- **Recommendations** — practical guidance for future sessions (e.g., "when doing X, use Y approach because Z")
27	- **Role insights** — if the session revealed something about how this agent's role should be understood differently, capture that as a separate role-update topic
28	
29	## How to Write Reflection Topics
30	
31	Write each reflection topic as a plain markdown file in the current agent's `reflections/` directory (the agent whose iteration is running). This directory lives **inside the agent's own directory** — `<lore-agent-repo>/agents/<agent-name>/reflections/`, a sibling of that agent's `lore/`, `lore-context.md`, and `workdir/`. It is **not** at the repo root and **not** at the workspace root. Create it if it does not exist. Writing reflections anywhere else (e.g. `<lore-agent-repo>/reflections/`) is wrong — the merge step, `/lr:check`, and finalization all look only inside the agent directory, so a misplaced reflection is silently lost.
32	
33	Rules:
34	- **One topic per file** — atomic, focused on a single concept or lesson
35	- **Filename**: lowercase kebab-case, descriptive (e.g., `api-retry-behavior.md`, `role-update-scope-change.md`)
36	- **Keep it compact** — only essential information, no filler or general knowledge
37	- **Be specific** — include concrete details, not vague observations
38	- **Include operational guidance** — if the knowledge implies a way of working, state it explicitly
39	
40	Role update topics should be named with a `role-update-` prefix so the merge process can identify them.
41	
42	## What NOT to Extract
43	
44	- Things that are obvious from the code or documentation
45	- General knowledge that anyone could look up
46	- Temporary state that won't matter next session
47	- Verbatim conversation excerpts — distill the insight, don't copy the chat
48	
49	## Example Reflection Topics
50	
51	**`api-timeout-handling.md`**:
52	```
53	The billing API has a 30-second timeout but does not return a timeout
54	error — it returns a generic 500. When retrying billing calls, treat
55	any 500 after 25+ seconds as a likely timeout rather than a server error.
56	Retry with the same idempotency key.
57	```
58	
59	**`role-update-deployment-ownership.md`**:
60	```
61	The team has decided that this agent should also handle deployment
62	verification after releases, not just the release process itself.
63	This includes checking health endpoints and monitoring error rates
64	for 15 minutes post-deploy.
65	```
66	
67	## After Reflection
68	
69	Once you have written all reflection topics for the current agent iteration, inform the user that reflection is complete for that agent and list the topics created. Then, if there are more active agents to reflect, move to the next one. When all active agents have reflected, announce that the full reflection step is complete.
70	
71	The next step — merging reflections into lore — is a separate step triggered by `/lr:merge`. Merge also iterates per active agent in the same host-first order.
72	
````

#### Result

```text
# Merge Process

Integrate reflection topics into the agent's existing lore.

## Execution model

Merge always runs in a subagent, one per active agent, with all subagents launched in parallel. This is uniform for single- and multi-agent sessions — a single-agent session just spawns one subagent. Running merge in a subagent keeps session noise out of merge decisions and gives a clean, focused context.

> **Engine note.** The spawn mechanics below describe Claude Code. If your engine profile (`<framework-root>/docs/engines/<engine>.md`, selected at boot) defines a **subagent-spawn override**, follow it instead — e.g. on Codex use `spawn_agent` (role `worker`), not the `Agent` tool, and the **host reads this procedure and passes the steps inline** into each subagent's brief rather than pointing the subagent at this doc. The subagent still boots as its target agent.

**Each subagent boots as the agent it is merging for**, using the standard boot procedure. Booting gives the subagent the agent's role, identity, and lore context naturally — the same pattern `/lr:consult` uses. After booting, the subagent runs the process below scoped to its own agent and returns a short summary to the host.

Host responsibilities when merge is invoked:

1. Collect active agents (host + any attached guests).
2. For each, spawn a **`general-purpose`** subagent (merge needs `Write`/`Edit`/`Bash`; `Explore` does not) with a brief such as: _"Boot as agent `<name>` (repo: `<path>`) per `<framework-root>/docs/agent-boot.md`, then run the merge procedure in `<framework-root>/docs/process-merge.md` scoped to yourself. Return a short summary of topics touched, role changes, and any anomalies. Do not commit — finalize handles that."_
3. In a multi-agent session, spawn all subagents in parallel (single message with multiple Agent tool calls).
4. Collect summaries and report per-agent success/failure to the user. **Retain each subagent's return** — phase 3 (summarize) uses it to compose guest summaries.

Each subagent's work is independent: separate `reflections/` directory, separate lore subtree. Merge does not commit — all changes are left uncommitted on disk. Committing is handled once, at the end of `/lr:finalize`, or by the user themselves if merge is invoked standalone.

If any subagent fails, the others still proceed.

## Inputs (per subagent)

- The agent's `lore-context.md` — current compacted knowledge
- The agent's `lore/` directory — existing lore topics
- The agent's `reflections/` directory — new reflection topics to integrate
- The agent's `role.md` — current role description

## Process

The steps below are what **each subagent** runs once booted as its target agent. The host does not run these steps inline — it orchestrates subagents per the Execution model above and aggregates their summaries.

### Step 0: Refresh the Repo

Before reading the lore for integration, run the auto-pull procedure in `<framework-root>/docs/auto-pull.md` against the agent's repo. The boot procedure already auto-pulls — this step is defense-in-depth, both because the freshness contract belongs at the merge site explicitly (the moment when stale lore is most damaging) and to cover any boot-pull skip (no remote, network blip) that may have left the repo behind.

`--ff-only` is safe even though the merge subagent's working tree is dirty (the `reflections/` from phase 1, or any merge-in-progress edits): git refuses to fast-forward if the operation would clobber uncommitted edits, and otherwise advances `HEAD` cleanly leaving the working tree untouched. See `docs/auto-pull.md` § Invariants.

If auto-pull surfaces a non-fast-forward failure, do **not** abort merge — proceed in degraded mode and surface the warning in your return summary so the host can flag it. Concurrent finalize collisions are handled by the push-conflict resolution path in `resolve-conflicts.md`, not here.

### Step 1: Read Everything

Read all reflection topics, the current `lore-context.md`, and scan the existing `lore/` directory to understand what's already there.

### Step 2: Integrate Lore Topics

For each reflection topic, decide:

1. **Update an existing topic** — if the reflection adds to, refines, or corrects an existing lore topic, update that topic in place. This is the preferred approach.

2. **Create a new topic** — if the reflection covers something no existing topic addresses, create a new file in `lore/`. Lowercase kebab-case filename.

3. **Remove an obsolete topic** — if a reflection makes an existing topic fundamentally wrong (not just partially outdated), delete it. Git preserves history.

When updating or creating topics:
- Keep topics under 5000 tokens when possible. Split if too large.
- Topics can reference other topics by filename.
- Summary topics can link to clusters of related topics.
- Include operational recommendations where relevant.
- Only essential information — no filler.

### Step 3: Handle Role Updates

If any reflection topic has a `role-update-` prefix:
- Read the current `role.md`
- Integrate the role update into the body
- Keep `role.md` focused and concise
- Preserve the YAML frontmatter. Update the `description` field if the role change warrants a different one-line summary.

### Step 4: Update Lore Context

Update `lore-context.md` to reflect the new state of the agent's knowledge.

**`lore-context.md` is the agent's every-session working knowledge and the entry point to the lore graph — not an index of all topics.** Carry the compacted knowledge needed in essentially every session, plus references to the most important / **summary** topics, and let the graph (summary topics → detail topics) carry the rest. Shape it accordingly:

- **Compacted working knowledge** — facts, decisions, and context the agent draws on across most sessions, stated tightly and in the **present tense**.
- **Reference summary topics; don't enumerate detail topics** — point at a theme's summary topic and let *it* fan out to the details. Do not list every file in `lore/`. If a significant theme has no summary topic to point at, **create one** (strengthening the graph's mid-tier) rather than inlining the whole cluster here.
- **No version-history narrative** — `lore-context.md` is present-tense. Changelog-style "vN did X" annotations and dated step-by-step history belong in git, release notes, or the relevant topic.

**Preserve graph navigability.** When you remove something from `lore-context.md`, ensure it still lives in a topic **and** is reachable from a summary topic that `lore-context.md` references — existence somewhere isn't enough; the entry point must still lead there.

**Size budget**: keep `lore-context.md` under 50,000 tokens — but shape is the primary discipline, not the ceiling. A well-shaped context sits comfortably under budget; nearing 50K is a drift signal that it has grown toward an index or accumulated history, so **restructure** (lift detail into topics, lean on summary topics, strip narrative) rather than merely summarizing older entries.

### Step 5: Cleanup

Delete the current agent's `reflections/` directory and all its contents.

Merge does not commit — leave all changes uncommitted on disk. In a multi-agent session, return a short summary of what was integrated (topics touched, role changes, any anomalies) to the host. Committing is handled at the end of `/lr:finalize`, or by the user directly if merge is invoked standalone.

## Guidelines

- **Preserve knowledge** — don't lose information during merging. If removing content from `lore-context.md`, make sure it exists in a lore topic.
- **Maintain the graph** — when creating new topics that relate to existing ones, add cross-references.
- **Be conservative with deletions** — only delete topics that are fundamentally wrong. Updating is almost always better.
- **Respect the agent's voice** — lore should read naturally as the agent's own knowledge, not as third-party documentation.
- **Shared topics** — the same topic may legitimately appear in multiple agents' lore if it matters to each of them. Don't try to consolidate into one agent's lore during merge; each agent owns its own copy for its own scope.
```

- model: `claude-sonnet-5`
